It is the canonical executor-OOM-on-shuffle scenario, its entire expectation was `effect: {module: join_and_aggregate}`, and that asserts only that a module the patch never touches still exists. It passed for every patch ever generated against it, including ones that changed nothing, and it would have kept passing after `set_engine_config` shipped without ever checking that the config write happened. Four separate holes made that possible and each is closed. **(1) The reader ignored keys it did not implement.** `load_scenario` read known keys with `raw.get(...)` and dropped the rest, so a typo'd `asertions:` graded the file against nothing at all: the format itself violated the no-silent-no-ops rule. Unknown keys are now refused by name at every level (top level, `inject_failure`, `expected_patch`, `effect`, each assertion mapping), along with an assertion nobody implements, a refusal reason outside the vocabulary, a gate a scenario never runs, and a `domains:` member outside the closed set. **This is not a format break and `aqueduct_scenario` stays `"1.0"`.** No 1.0 file was ever documented as being allowed to carry an unrecognised key, so a file this now refuses was already being mis-graded; and keeping the old permissiveness for files declaring `"1.0"` would preserve exactly the silent no-op the tightening exists to end, which would make a `"1.1"` reader a byte-identical code path under a second name. Every key added here is optional and additive, so a correct 1.0 file grades identically. The version field earns a bump the day a change is genuinely not additive, which is when a reader has to branch on it. Malformed files raise `ScenarioError` (a typed `AqueductError`, not a bare `ValueError`) because a benchmark suite is something a user writes and ships; `aqueduct doctor --aqscenario` branches on that type and reports the loader's own message, which names the offending key and the legal set, rather than burying it under a generic "load failed". **(2) `expected_patch.effect` had no way to state a config fix.** It could only name a module and grade that module's config, so a `set_engine_config` patch, which touches no module, had no legal shape at all: `module:` is no longer required, and the block gained `engine_config` (the post-patch value of a key), `engine_config_changed` (a key moved, without pinning to what) and `any_of` (any one of several acceptable fixes). Engine-config keys are addressed as `{engine: {key: value}}` through the same `blueprint_engine_layers` normaliser Gate 1 uses, so a scenario addresses Spark's free-form `conf` bag and DuckDB's typed fields identically and never has to know which its target engine uses. Their VALUES compare by equality on the canonical string form rather than by substring: every engine-config value reaches the session as a string, so `200` and `"200"` are the same setting, while a substring rule would let an actual of `1200` satisfy an expected `200`, which is the superstring bug `config_contains` already had to fix for numbers. An effect block stating no expectation at all is now a hard error, since a block that expects nothing passes for free and that is the defect in one line. **(3) Four different refusals were reported as one bit.** `patch_applies: false` means "it did not apply" and covers a malformed patch, a Blueprint-guardrail violation, a Gate 1 engine-config POLICY refusal and an INERT config write, whose fixes are respectively a different patch, a different op, a different key and a different value. A new `patch_refused: <reason>` states which, classified by exception TYPE (`EngineConfigPolicyError` / `EngineConfigInertError` / `PatchError` / `ParseError`+`CompileError`) and never by matching a message, and a new `gate_status: {engine_config: <status>}` asserts Gate 1's own verdict in the shared `GateStatus` vocabulary. Both are separate keys rather than a reason bolted onto `patch_applies`, which would have changed an existing key's value TYPE from a boolean to a mapping and broken every scenario in the wild while still leaving a plain `patch_applies: false` asserting nothing about why; they are checked independently, so a scenario may state both and neither can be silently satisfied by the other. Only `engine_config` is assertable, because a scenario starts no engine session and the lineage, sandbox and explain gates therefore never run, and a gate that did not run has no verdict: asserting a status on a patch the allowlist refused before the delta gate was reached fails loudly rather than resolving to one. **(4) The apply that produces the evidence only happened as a side effect.** `_try_apply_patch` was called from inside the `patch_applies` assertion, so a scenario stating an `expected_patch.effect` and no `patch_applies` graded its effect against `patched_dict=None`, silently, and passed. `run_scenario` now applies once, up front, and every consumer reads that one attempt. Two things ride along that the previous shape could not carry: the PRE-patch Blueprint dict, without which `engine_config_changed` compared against an empty "before" and read every present key as a change; and the scenario's `engine`, which now reaches the post-patch re-compile, so a DuckDB scenario is graded against DuckDB's capability table instead of Spark's. `_try_apply_patch` returns a frozen `ApplyOutcome` rather than a positional tuple, because `applied=False` with no refusal (never attempted) and `applied=False` with `refusal="policy"` (the gate said no) are different states and a tuple makes them one index apart. The other half of that hole is closed with it: applying up front produces the evidence, but the grader still returned "no failures" when there was no post-patch Blueprint to grade against, so a stated effect whose patch Gate 1 refused was scored PASS for the same reason as before. An effect that could not be graded is now a failure naming the refusal — one line, not a cascade of per-key noise on top of it, which is why the branch skipped grading in the first place — while a scenario stating no effect at all is untouched, since `12` and `13` are exactly that shape. **Scenarios declare `domains:`, and `aqueduct benchmark --domain` selects on it.** The vocabulary is closed (`pipeline`, `engine_config`) and named rather than numbered, since a number implies an ordering and a count that do not exist. Filing scenarios into per-domain FOLDERS was considered and rejected: domain is a property of the FIX, not of the failure, and scenario 07 is fixable either by raising the shuffle-partition count or by inserting a repartition step, so a folder would assert the answer the scenario exists to test. A scenario may declare both, and 07 does. A `--domain` value outside the vocabulary is a click usage error naming the legal set rather than a filter that matches nothing; a scenario declaring no domains is excluded by any filter and reported by id, so a filtered suite never shrinks in silence; and a filter that selects nothing exits `DATA_OR_RUNTIME` instead of reporting success after running zero pairs. `--format json` gained `refusal` and `engine_config_gate` per pair, without which a failed config-heal pair reads only as `patch_applies: false`. **Scenario 07 now grades the fix**, with `any_of` over the two legitimate repairs and `engine_config_changed` rather than a pinned partition count, because the right number is a property of the cluster and nothing here can see one; "the key moved" is still a real assertion because Gate 1 refuses a write whose effective config is identical before and after. Four scenarios join it, each with its own Blueprint: `11_driver_max_result_size` (an allowlisted write with a real effective delta, asserting `gate_status: pass`), `12_engine_config_denied_key` (the engine's own advice names a denied JVM-options key; the scenario passes when Gate 1 refuses it, so it grades the gate rather than the model), `13_engine_config_inert_write` (the suggested value is the one already configured; refused as inert, `gate_status: fail`), and `14_duckdb_memory_limit` (`inject_failure.engine: duckdb`, a typed `engine.duckdb.memory_limit` field rather than a Spark `conf` key, covering the engine-agnostic path that nothing else exercised). Every one of them was checked against the patch it is written for AND against every wrong patch, through the real gates with no mock and no LLM call, because a scenario that cannot fail is not a test. **Every gallery scenario now declares its domains, and scenario 09 turned out to have 07's defect in a second spelling.** Nine of the fourteen declared none, and an undeclared scenario is excluded by every filter, so `--domain pipeline` selected one of the nine scenarios that are pipeline fixes — the flag worked and the suite quietly shrank. `09_broadcast_join_timeout` expected `config_contains: {query: ""}`, an empty substring against a Channel that was already built with a `query`: every string is a superstring of `""`, so it asserted that a key which was never absent is still present. It now grades `engine_config_changed` on `spark.sql.autoBroadcastJoinThreshold`. It grades only that route, and says so in the file: the error text names a second legitimate fix — delete the `/*+ BROADCAST(...) */` hint — which the effect grammar cannot express, because `config_contains` asks whether a substring is PRESENT and there is no way to ask whether one is gone. A model taking that route fails the scenario; declaring `domains: [pipeline]` as well would claim coverage the file does not have. Two guards keep both defects from returning: one fails if any shipped scenario declares no domains, the other reads each scenario's pre-patch Blueprint and fails on an empty-substring expectation for a key the Blueprint ALREADY carries — deliberately not on the spelling alone, since scenario 10 expects `coalesce: ""` on an Egress that has no `coalesce`, where "present afterwards" is a real claim about the fix. **Then three live benchmark runs against a real model rewrote two of these scenarios and added a matcher to the grammar.** `config_not_contains` is the format's only negative matcher, and it exists because a whole class of pipeline fixes is a REMOVAL — delete a join hint, drop a bad reader option — which no positive matcher can express. Scenario 09's second legitimate fix is deleting a `/*+ BROADCAST(...) */` hint; the model took exactly that route three times out of three, produced a correct patch each time, and was scored FAIL, because the scenario could only ask whether a substring was PRESENT. A benchmark that fails the answer a model actually gives is worse than one that is merely incomplete. The matcher is case-INSENSITIVE and raw, both deliberately: SQL hint syntax is case-tolerant, so a case-sensitive rule would report a hint as removed while it is still there — a false PASS in the one direction a negative matcher must never fail — and SQL normalisation can drop a comment-borne hint while parsing, deleting the very text being searched for. It requires `module:` exactly as `config_contains` does, refuses an empty needle (every value contains it, so the assertion could never fail), and treats a key missing from the config as satisfied, removal being a stronger form of the same claim. It is paired with a positive expectation in 09, because on its own it would pass for a patch that deleted the query outright. **Scenarios 12 and 13 are inverted.** Both asserted `patch_refused:` — they required the model to TAKE the engine's bait, a denied JVM-options key in one and an echo of an already-configured value in the other. But the healing prompt discloses the whole allowlist AND the deny families, so a model that reads it routes around both traps: three runs out of three it did, once writing its reasoning into the patch's own notes. They measured model gullibility and scored WORSE as models improved, which is the wrong direction for a benchmark row, and they could only reach the gate they were grading if the model misbehaved. They now grade the routing-around — the property disclosure exists to produce. The refusal paths lose no coverage: `EngineConfigPolicyError` and `EngineConfigInertError` are raised deterministically and asserted by the suites over `patch/config_delta.py`, the allowlist loader and the gate simulation. Nor does the inversion cost the security direction: asserting only `gate_status: pass` would still hold if core's deny layer regressed and admitted `extraJavaOptions`, so the expectation names allowlisted memory knobs, and a write that clears every gate while landing on the wrong key still fails. (`aqueduct/surveyor/scenario.py`, `aqueduct/errors.py`, `aqueduct/cli/benchmark.py`, `aqueduct/doctor/checks_io.py`, `aqueduct/patch/apply.py`, `aqueduct/patch/config_delta.py`, `gallery/aqscenarios/07_spark_oom_shuffle.aqscenario.yml`, four new `gallery/aqscenarios/*.aqscenario.yml` + their `blueprints/`, `gallery/README.md`, `gallery/aqscenarios/README.md`, `docs/cli_reference.md`, `docs/specs.md` §8.11; tests: `tests/test_surveyor/test_scenario.py`, `tests/test_cli/test_cli_benchmark.py`)
- **`aqueduct patch revert` — a supported way to undo a healed config patch, and a loud refusal for every case it cannot make safe.** Perf attribution shipped the ability to OBSERVE that a healed `set_engine_config` patch left runs slower; there was no way to act on the observation. The patch persists into the Blueprint through `healed_by:` and every later run inherits it, and the only remedies were hand-editing the YAML (which desynchronises the provenance record from the file it describes) or `aqueduct patch rollback`, which needs the patch to have been git-committed and restores the whole file, later unrelated edits included. The new verb undoes one patch's engine-config writes in place: each `engine.<name>` key goes back to the value that patch's own `engine_config_delta` captured before it was applied, the pre-revert Blueprint is backed up under `patches/backups/`, and the document is re-parsed before it replaces the live file — the same order `patch apply` uses. **What it will not do is the design.** Only engine-config writes are revertible, because they are the only change for which a prior value is recorded anywhere: `set_module_config_key` and its siblings store the new value alone, so a module patch has no inverse to compute. So the applied patch BODY is required as proof the patch was config-only (the `healed_by` record cannot tell a config-only patch from a mixed one), and a patch carrying any other op is refused by name rather than half-undone — reverting the config half of a mixed patch would leave a Blueprint matching no state that ever ran, which is worse than no revert verb at all. Refused for the same reason: a key a later, not-itself-reverted patch overwrote (reverting patch 2 of 3 underneath patch 3); a value edited since the heal, where reverting would silently overwrite a human's change; a patch id matching zero or more than one record (model-authored ids are not unique, and Aqueduct will not guess which application to undo); and a recorded prior state of "absent" that removal can no longer reach because `aqueduct.yml` has since gained the key. The last check is mechanical rather than argued: the plan is applied to a copy and re-resolved through `resolve_effective_engine_configs` — the SAME function Gate 1 measured the delta with — and the revert aborts unless every reverted key lands exactly on its recorded prior value AND no key the patch never wrote moves at all. Every refusal is a `RevertError`, a direct `AqueductError` subclass and deliberately NOT a `PatchError`: "this applied patch cannot be safely undone" and "this patch cannot be applied" are different states whose fixes differ, and a handler written for one must not swallow the other. **The patch grammar gains no op.** It is permanently closed, and a revert could not use it anyway: the inverse of a write whose recorded `before` was absent is a key DELETION, which no op expresses. What does fit goes through the real dispatch — a restore-to-a-value is executed as a synthesized `SetEngineConfigOp` through `apply_set_engine_config`, so key addressing (Spark's `conf` bag vs DuckDB's typed fields) stays answered in exactly one place — and the deletion half is a plain Blueprint rewrite, which is the concrete reason a revert is a rewrite rather than a patch. It also never becomes a heal: no `healed_by` record is appended, no `patch_index` lifecycle slot is taken, no model-authored `patch_id`/`rationale` is invented. **The provenance record is annotated, not deleted.** Deleting it would erase the fact that a heal ever happened — the thing `healed_by:` exists to carry — and leaving it untouched would make it describe a Blueprint that no longer contains its change, so the record gains a `reverted_at:` timestamp and stays. All three consumers of the block were traced and now read it: the cross-engine compile gate (`check_cross_engine_heal`) stops warning about a record whose dialect-bearing content is no longer in the file; `stamp_validated_engine` stops appending engines to it, because a green run validates nothing about a patch that was not in the Blueprint it ran; `stamp_perf_observation` stops writing notes to it and stops counting it as a co-applied patch sharing another record's duration. (`aqueduct/patch/revert.py` [new], `aqueduct/cli/patch.py`, `aqueduct/patch/apply.py`, `aqueduct/compiler/capability_check.py`, `aqueduct/parser/schema.py`, `aqueduct/parser/models.py`, `aqueduct/parser/parser.py`, `aqueduct/patch/config_delta.py`, both engines' `capabilities.yml`, `docs/specs.md`, `docs/cli_reference.md`, `docs/compatibility.md`, `SKILL.md`, `AGENTS.md`, `aqueduct/templates/default/blueprints/blueprint.yml.template`; tests: `tests/test_patch/test_revert.py`, `tests/test_cli/test_cli_patch_revert.py`)
- **`aqueduct doctor` now surfaces the healed engine-config keys a Blueprint carries — with the facts, and deliberately without a staleness threshold.** A healed `set_engine_config` write outlives the failure it was written for, and nothing made those keys visible short of reading the `healed_by:` block by hand. Doctor now emits one `healed-config:<patch_id>` row per record carrying an `engine_config_delta`, naming what was changed and when, on which engine, whether any green run has validated it, what each perf note measured, and the exact `aqueduct patch revert` command that undoes it. **No staleness threshold ships, and no knob for one either.** "Healed more than N days ago" and "more than N runs ago" are both numbers nothing in the host, the schema, or any measurement supports: an hour-old heal on a pipeline that runs monthly is older, in every sense that matters, than a year-old one on a pipeline that runs hourly. Relocating the invention into a user-settable knob would not avoid it — it would hand the same unmeasured guess to someone with no more evidence, while adding a config field, its `engine_scoped` tag, a capability leaf on every engine, and docs. Perf ratios are printed verbatim with the standing statement that Aqueduct sets no regression threshold, exactly as the note itself does. The ONE condition that escalates to `warn` is not a threshold but an equality that either holds or does not: the value the record says the patch wrote is no longer what the effective session config resolves to. That has concrete consequences worth a human's attention — the record's perf attribution no longer describes the live configuration, and `patch revert` will refuse the record for the same reason — and it is checked by comparing two recorded values, not by picking a magnitude. A Blueprint with no healed engine-config keys produces no rows at all, the same "nothing to say" shape `check_cascade_tiers` already uses. (`aqueduct/doctor/__init__.py`, `docs/specs.md`, `AGENTS.md`; tests: `tests/test_cli/test_cli_doctor_healed_config.py`)
- **A healed patch now reports what it cost, not only that it ran — warn-only perf attribution.** `healed_by.validated_on` is binary: the run after a patch either succeeded or it did not. Config-op success is not binary. The usual outcome of naive shuffle or partition tuning is a pipeline that still completes and is three times slower, and `validated_on` recorded that as an unqualified success while the patch persisted into the Blueprint and every later run silently inherited it. Two new machine-written `healed_by:` fields carry the half that was missing. `perf_baseline` is snapshotted at apply time — the last green run of that blueprint finishing before the patch, its wall-clock duration from `run_records` plus an input-volume proxy summed from `module_metrics` — snapshotted rather than looked up later because the Blueprint travels and the observability store does not. `perf_observations` is written by the same green-run stamp that already appends to `validated_on`, once per engine, so it is bounded by the engine count rather than growing with every run, and each record is compared against its OWN baseline so two patches applied at different times produce two genuinely different comparisons. **No threshold ships, deliberately.** There is no measurement behind a number like "3x is a regression" — the ratio at which a change matters depends on the pipeline, the SLA, and the cluster, none of which this code can see — so the note reports `duration_ratio`, both raw durations, and stops. Its status vocabulary is `observed` or `not_applicable`, following `LineageGateResult`/`EngineConfigDeltaResult`: there is no `pass`, because nothing was judged, and no `fail`, because nothing can fail. It never blocks a run, never changes acceptance, and never touches an exit code; the run prints a dim informational line (a new `emit_info` funnel state in `aqueduct/cli/output.py` — a `⚠` would claim a verdict this deliberately refuses to make) and a human decides. Comparability is handled in the output rather than assumed: a baseline whose engine set differs from the observing run's is refused outright as `not_applicable` instead of compared, with the engine set derived from the per-module `engine` that `run_records.module_results` already records (no new column — the fact was already there); the volume proxy comes from `module_metrics`, which Spark's executor writes per module and DuckDB's writes only for Handoff modules, so on DuckDB it is reported as unavailable with a stated reason rather than as a zero or an omitted row that would read as "volume unchanged"; and every remaining limit — co-applied patches sharing the delta, changed input volume, the standing fact that wall-clock time has many causes — is written into the note's own `caveats`, which travel with it into the Blueprint, because a note implying causation it cannot support is worse than no note. Both engines' `capabilities.yml` declare the two new leaves with hints naming the per-engine volume-proxy difference. (`aqueduct/patch/perf_attribution.py` (new), `aqueduct/patch/apply.py`, `aqueduct/patch/provenance.py`, `aqueduct/parser/schema.py`, `aqueduct/parser/models.py`, `aqueduct/parser/parser.py`, `aqueduct/cli/run.py`, `aqueduct/cli/__init__.py`, `aqueduct/cli/output.py`, both `capabilities.yml`, `docs/specs.md` §8.14 → Version 2.61, `SKILL.md`, `blueprint.yml.template`; tests: `tests/test_patch/test_perf_attribution.py`, `tests/test_cli/test_output.py`)
- **A `set_engine_config` patch that changes nothing is now refused instead of recorded as applied.** The config an engine actually runs with is a merge (`resolve_session_engine_config`): that engine's `aqueduct.yml` `engine.<name>` block with the Blueprint's own layered on top. So a write of the value that already resolves — from `aqueduct.yml`, or from the Blueprint itself — applied perfectly cleanly and changed nothing: schema valid, allowlist clean, lineage `not_applicable`, sandbox `pass`, patch archived, `healed_by:` stamped, and the next run failing identically because no engine ever saw a different setting. Nothing anywhere said so. That is the silent no-op this codebase forbids, made worse by costing a heal attempt while reporting success. Gate 1 (`aqueduct/patch/apply.py::_check_guardrails`) now resolves the effective session config before and after the patch and raises `PatchError` when a patch writes engine config but the resolved config is identical on both sides — `PatchError`, not `EngineConfigAllowlistError`, for the same reason an allowlist violation is: the shipped data is fine, the patch is the thing that has to change. Values are compared the way a session sees them (every engine-config value ends up a string), so re-spelling `400` as `"400"` is correctly read as no change rather than as a delta. Because Gate 1 is the one function every apply path calls, the check covers `aqueduct run` self-heal, `aqueduct heal`, `patch preview`, `patch apply`/`patch import`, and benchmark scenario replay without a per-path opt-in; `cfg` is a required keyword-only argument there, so a future call site that forgets it is a `TypeError`, not a quietly weakened gate, and an AST scan over `aqueduct/` fails the build if one appears.
- **Applicability is derived from what the patch writes, not from a list of "config ops".** An include-list of operation names is a codified bug shape here — it stops covering the next op the day someone adds one. Instead the patch's operations are re-applied to a copy of the Blueprint whose `engine:` block has been removed, and the block is read back: whatever is in it is exactly what this patch writes into engine config, obtained through the real op dispatch. Stripping first is what makes the answer independent of whether the value changed — the most obvious no-op of all, re-writing what the Blueprint already carries, leaves the document byte-identical, so a plain before/after diff would have classified it as "this patch has no config surface". A patch that writes no engine config reports `not_applicable`, following the vocabulary `LineageGateResult` established: `pass` for a check that never ran is a lie. `aqueduct patch preview` renders the gate beside the lineage/sandbox/explain gates and carries it in `--format json` as `engine_config`.
- **The effective-config delta is recorded in `healed_by:`.** Applied patches whose write does change something now carry `engine_config_delta: {engine: {key: {before, after}}}` in their `healed_by:` provenance record (a new optional `HealedByRecordSchema` field). It belongs there rather than in the patch's own `_aq_meta` because it is an apply-time fact: the same patch applied against a different `aqueduct.yml` produces a different delta, and the model that wrote the patch saw neither — `_aq_meta` documents how a patch was produced, `healed_by:` documents what applying it did. Landing it in the Blueprint also puts the YAML write and the behaviour it bought in a single `git diff`. Absent, not empty, for a patch that writes no engine config, so a pipeline-only heal's provenance block is byte-identical to before.

- **The healing model is now told the whole `set_engine_config` policy, instead of discovering it through Gate 1 rejections.** The op was gated at Gate 1 against each engine's `engine_config_allowlist.yml`, but the string `set_engine_config` appeared nowhere in the prompt layer — not in the scaffold, not in either engine's `PromptRules` pack — so the only way the model learned a key was refused was by having a patch thrown out. That turns a policy gate into a discovery mechanism and burns heal attempts on guesses. The composed system prompt now carries an "Engine/session config" section rendering the TARGET engine's complete allowlist: every allow entry with its type and any `enum`/`range`, every deny entry with its mandatory `reason`. Whole list, never a curated subset — Spark's allow section is ~13 rows, so a "top N keys" split would buy nothing and drift the moment a row is added; a test compares the rendered prompt against the shipped YAML so a new row must reach the model too. An engine that ships no allowlist (or an explicitly empty one) is told the op is UNAVAILABLE rather than shown an empty table: `set_engine_config` can never pass Gate 1 there, and an empty section would leave the model believing it may write engine config and be refused every time. Composition stays engine-agnostic — the agent layer loads the allowlist through the same core, name-keyed loader Gate 1 uses, adds no field to `PromptRules`/`ExecutorProtocol`, and imports no engine module; a guard test proves engine X's prompt never carries engine Y's keys. `PROMPT_VERSION` 1.10 → 1.11. (`aqueduct/agent/prompts.py`, `aqueduct/agent/loop.py`, `docs/specs.md`; tests: `tests/test_agent/test_prompt_composition.py`, `tests/test_agent/test_prompts.py`)
- **`aqueduct patch policy [--engine <name>] [--format text|json]` — see what the healing agent may write.** Gate 1 refuses a `set_engine_config` patch whose key is denied, unlisted or wrongly typed, but until now nothing could show a user what *is* allowed: the policy ships inside `site-packages`, no command printed it, no doc listed it. A rejection naming a rule nobody can look up is half a gate. The command prints each registered engine's allowed key patterns with their type and any enum or range, and the denied families **with their reasons** — the reasons being the part that lets a human decide whether to argue with the policy. It reads the same `engine_config_allowlist.yml` Gate 1 enforces, so the two cannot disagree, and it states plainly that operator extension and narrowing are not implemented, rather than implying configurability that does not exist. Gate 1's rejection messages now name the command, and tests on both sides guard that link against drift. (`aqueduct/cli/patch.py`, `aqueduct/patch/apply.py`, `docs/cli_reference.md`, `docs/specs.md`; tests: `tests/test_cli/test_cli_patch_policy.py`, `tests/test_patch/test_guardrails_rollback.py`)
- **Gate 1 enforces the engine-config allowlist, closing the gap where `set_engine_config` was ungated.** The allowlist and deny layer shipped, but nothing consumed them: `_check_guardrails` checked only `forbidden_ops` and `allowed_paths`, and `forbidden_ops` defaults to empty — so on `approval: auto` a heal could write any engine config key at all, including ones that redirect where work runs or load code into the JVM. Every `set_engine_config` op is now evaluated against the target engine's allowlist: deny first (the rejection carries the deny entry's own reason), then allow membership (a key on no list is refused — fail closed), then type, then range/enum. The check runs unconditionally, before the `allowed_paths` short-circuit, so an unset `allowed_paths` cannot skip it, and it covers every apply path (`run`, `heal`, `patch preview`, `patch apply`, benchmark replay) because Gate 1 is shared. Error types stay distinct: a patch violating policy raises `PatchError` (the fix is a different patch), while a missing or malformed shipped allowlist raises `EngineConfigAllowlistError` (the fix is data or a reinstall). An unregistered engine also fails closed. `SetEngineConfigOp`'s docstring claimed the op was "default-forbidden in auto mode" — never true, since the template ships that line commented out and no code implemented a default — and now describes what actually constrains it. The permission model is documented in `docs/specs.md` §8 and as a rule in `AGENTS.md`: every layer below core may only subtract permission; nothing but the core allowlist and a future operator extension ever adds. (`aqueduct/patch/apply.py`, `aqueduct/executor/engine_config_allowlist.py`, `aqueduct/patch/grammar.py`, `docs/specs.md`, `AGENTS.md`; tests: `tests/test_patch/test_guardrails_rollback.py`, `tests/test_executor/test_engine_config_allowlist.py`)
- **A core deny layer in `engine_config_allowlist.yml`, and every invented magnitude range deleted from it.** The exclusion families were YAML comments, so nothing structurally stopped a future entry — or a future operator extension — from allowlisting `spark.master`. They are now enforced `deny:` rows carrying a mandatory `reason:` that will render into the rejection. Spark ships four families: placement (`spark.master`, `spark.submit.*`, `spark.kubernetes.*`, `spark.yarn.*`, `spark.mesos.*`), credentials and endpoints (`spark.hadoop.*`, `*.jars.repositories`), safety-disable (`spark.ssl.*`, `spark.network.crypto.*`, `spark.authenticate*`, plus a value-level deny of `maxResultSize: 0`), and **code loading** (`spark.jars`, `spark.jars.packages`, `spark.files`, `spark.submit.pyFiles`, `spark.plugins`, `spark.sql.extensions`, and the `extraClassPath`/`extraJavaOptions`/`extraLibraryPath` families — `extraJavaOptions` alone admits `-javaagent:`). That last family was absent even from the prose, and it is the no-arbitrary-code-execution moat itself. DuckDB denies `database_path`, `extension_repository` and `s3_*` even though none are Blueprint-level today, so a future schema addition cannot silently make them healable. `load_allowlist()` refuses a file where an absolute-ban deny pattern is reachable through an allow entry, so a regression fails CI rather than a live heal, and `validate_against_deny()` pins the contract a future operator-extension surface must call. The deny layer is immutable by construction: no function in the module takes a parameter that could merge, shrink or override it. Separately, every `range:` is gone from both engines — a bound core cannot derive is a bound core must not state, since `["128m","64g"]` blocked a legitimate 96g heal and admitted an absurd 64g one. The `range:`/`enum:` mechanism stays in the loader for a future operator-narrowing feature; core simply ships none. `maxResultSize`'s old `1m` floor was safety reasoning rather than a magnitude guess and moved to the deny layer; the broadcast `1 GiB` cap was a deployment fact and is gone with no replacement. (`aqueduct/executor/engine_config_allowlist.py`, both `engine_config_allowlist.yml` files; tests: `tests/test_executor/test_engine_config_allowlist.py`)
- **Per-engine healing config allowlist — `engine_config_allowlist.yml`, shipped beside each engine's `capabilities.yml`.** Names which `engine.<name>` keys a `set_engine_config` patch may write, with a value type and an optional range or enum, because membership alone would admit `spark.executor.memory: 2000g` — a value no sandbox replay ever allocates, so it would pass every gate and fail in production. This closes a real hole: the Blueprint-level engine block carries no capability leaf at all (the grammar-leaf walker never covered the engine block schemas, so Spark's `engine.spark.conf` has none either), which makes this allowlist the only constraint on what the model may write there. Entries follow the shape of the block they address — glob prefixes for Spark's free-form `conf` bag, exact field names for DuckDB's typed block, told apart by the same "does this schema declare a `conf` field" question the parser and the patch op already use, never a hardcoded engine name. Spark ships shuffle partitioning, broadcast threshold, executor/driver memory and overhead, adaptive execution, max result size, partition sizing and Kryo buffers; the files document what is deliberately excluded and why — anything redirecting where work runs (`spark.master`, deploy mode, cluster URLs), credentials and endpoints, and anything whose effect is to disable a safety property. DuckDB ships its two Blueprint-level knobs. The loader (`aqueduct/executor/engine_config_allowlist.py`, pyspark-free) hard-validates at load and raises `EngineConfigAllowlistError`, a fourth capability-adjacent error type kept a sibling of the existing three so no handler can swallow the wrong one. Every registered engine must ship a file; an empty one is allowed and means "this engine heals no config", an absent one is an error, so the decision is visible rather than a gap nobody noticed. Enforcement against a live patch is the next step — this is data and loader only. (`aqueduct/executor/engine_config_allowlist.py`, `aqueduct/executor/spark/engine_config_allowlist.yml`, `aqueduct/executor/duckdb_/engine_config_allowlist.yml`, `aqueduct/errors.py`; tests: `tests/test_executor/test_engine_config_allowlist.py`)
- **A Blueprint can now set `engine.duckdb.memory_limit` / `engine.duckdb.threads` per pipeline.** `DuckDBEngineBlockSchema` had stayed empty because a Blueprint had no consumer to override — the per-engine `engine_config` remediation gave it one, since `resolve_session_engine_config` now layers a Blueprint's per-engine entry over the `aqueduct.yml` config for every registered engine. Blueprint wins on a conflict; an unset field leaves the `aqueduct.yml` value (or DuckDB's own default) untouched, which is what `model_dump(exclude_unset=True)` in the parser guarantees. Both knobs reach the same `SET memory_limit` / `SET threads` path the engine-level config already used (`executor/duckdb_/engine.py::_make_session`). Deployment and connection fields (`database_path`, `extension_repository`, `s3_*`) stay `aqueduct.yml`-only: they describe how this installation connects, not what a pipeline needs, the same reasoning `SparkEngineBlockSchema` documents for omitting `master_url`. These fields add no capability leaf — the Blueprint-level engine block has never been part of the grammar-leaf walker (Spark's `engine.spark.conf` has no leaf either), and the underlying capability is already governed at `config.engine.duckdb.*`. (`aqueduct/parser/schema.py`, `templates/default/blueprints/blueprint.yml.template`, `docs/specs.md` → Version 2.54, `SKILL.md`; tests: `tests/test_parser/test_engine_block_resolution.py`, `tests/test_executor_duckdb/test_engine_config.py`)
- **Catalog `table:` addressing runs on the DuckDB engine, for both Ingress and Egress (Pass G2).** `feature.table_addressing`'s old hint claimed DuckDB has no catalog to resolve a bare `table:` name against — measurably false: `SELECT DISTINCT catalog_name FROM duckdb_databases()` on a real connection lists `memory`/`system` plus whatever `ATTACH` adds, a genuine three-part `catalog.schema.table` namespace. What was missing was the implementation, now built: Ingress reads via `con.table(table)` (`duckdb_/ingress.py::_read_table`); Egress writes via `CREATE OR REPLACE TABLE`/plain `CREATE TABLE`/`CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS`/`INSERT INTO ... BY NAME`, mode-mapped onto DuckDB's own DDL guards rather than a manual existence check (`duckdb_/egress.py::_write_table`) — `overwrite`/`error`/`errorifexists`/`ignore`/`append`, the same mode vocabulary path-based writes already support. Defaulting rule (documented in both functions' docstrings): an unqualified name resolves against the connection's current catalog+schema (`memory.main` by default); a two-part name resolves its schema within the current catalog; a three-part name addresses a specific catalog directly, which must already exist — Aqueduct never performs an implicit `ATTACH`, the same division of responsibility Spark's `table:` has with `engine.spark.conf`'s `spark.sql.catalog.*` keys. An unresolvable name is DuckDB's own `CatalogException`, wrapped into `IngressError`/`EgressError`, never a silent miss. `register_as_table` also now runs on DuckDB: `CREATE OR REPLACE VIEW` over the written file (`read_parquet`/`read_csv`) — a live pointer to the file's current contents, not a snapshot copy, the same external-registration semantics as Spark's `CREATE EXTERNAL TABLE ... LOCATION`; non-fatal on failure, and ignored (with a warning, never silently) when `table:` is also set. `feature.table_addressing`, `ingress.field.table`, `egress.field.table`, and `egress.field.register_as_table` all flip from `unsupported` to `supported`. Gallery snippet `35_register_as_table` — UNSUPPORTED on the DuckDB lane purely because of this leaf — now runs end to end on both engines; while verifying it, found and fixed a real, independent defect (AGENTS.md "fix the defect you found"): the blueprint had no filter step at all despite its own README claiming step 2 "filters to in-stock items" — added the missing `Channel: op: filter` module so the demonstrated behavior matches what's documented, on both engines. `23_table_first` (Ingress `table:`/Egress `table:`, no `register_as_table`) also now compiles clean on DuckDB. (`aqueduct/executor/duckdb_/ingress.py`, `aqueduct/executor/duckdb_/egress.py`, `aqueduct/executor/duckdb_/capabilities.yml`, `gallery/snippets/35_register_as_table/blueprint.yml`, `docs/specs.md` §4.4 → Version 2.49, `docs/compatibility.md` regenerated; tests: `tests/test_executor_duckdb/test_executor.py` [12 new], `tests/test_capabilities/test_table_addressing.py`)
- **Per-signal-type `probe.signal.*` capability leaves (Pass G2).** A Probe's `config.signals[].type` was invisible to the capability framework — `probe_signal.field.type` gated the whole field as one leaf, so DuckDB's genuine gap (`partition_stats` has no single-process equivalent) was only caught at RUNTIME (`runtime_probe_signal_unsupported`) instead of at compile time like every other unsupported feature, the same freeform-value blind spot `channel.op.*`/`ingress.format.*`/`egress.mode.*` were built to close for their own fields. New `aqueduct/executor/probe_plugins.py::BUILTIN_SIGNAL_TYPES` — the closed vocabulary of the 9 built-in signal types (`custom` excluded: a user-code escape valve with its own compile-time shape check, not an enumerable per-engine capability) — sourced as a curated constant beside the dispatch code, same mechanism `channel_ops.py::ALL_OPS` uses, rather than a pydantic `Literal` on `ProbeSignalSchema.type` (which would need its own hand-maintained copy of the same list, since the parser layer must not import the executor layer). New `probe.signal.<type>` leaf family in `capability_leaves.py`, checked per-module in `compiler/capability_check.py::leaves_for_module`. Spark declares all nine `supported`; DuckDB declares eight `supported` and `probe.signal.partition_stats` `unsupported` with the same "no partition concept" hint as before, now enforced by the compile-time gate rather than only the runtime warning (which stays as a deliberate backstop for a caller that reaches `execute_probe` directly, bypassing `compile()`). (`aqueduct/executor/probe_plugins.py`, `aqueduct/executor/capability_leaves.py`, `aqueduct/compiler/capability_check.py`, `aqueduct/executor/duckdb_/probe.py`, `aqueduct/executor/spark/capabilities.yml`, `aqueduct/executor/duckdb_/capabilities.yml`, `docs/specs.md` §4.4/§10.9, `docs/compatibility.md` regenerated; tests: `tests/test_capabilities/test_gate_module_and_feature.py`, `tests/test_executor/test_executor_probe.py` [7 new], `tests/test_capabilities/test_closure.py`)
- **The Probe module now runs on the DuckDB engine (Pass F).** Probe was declared `unsupported` purely as a scope decision (`module.type.Probe`'s hint said outright "the DuckDB handlers cover Ingress/Channel/Junction/Funnel/Egress/Assert only"); most signals are plain SQL on a lazy relation. New `aqueduct/executor/duckdb_/probe.py` implements 8 of Spark's 9 built-in signal types — `schema_snapshot` (zero query, metadata only), `null_rates`, `value_distribution`, `distinct_count`, `data_freshness` (each a SQL aggregate, optionally over a `USING SAMPLE ... (bernoulli)` sample, same register/query/unregister idiom `duckdb_/assert_.py`'s `null_rate` rule already uses), `sample_rows` (`rel.limit(n)`), `threshold` (a sqlglot-transpiled Spark-SQL boolean aggregate — the Regulator-gate signal), and `custom` (reusing the shared, engine-agnostic `probe_plugins.py` resolver unmodified — inline SQL transpiled the same way as `threshold`, or a `module:`/`plugin:` callable resolved through `infra/module_loading`, never a bare `importlib.import_module`). **`row_count_estimate` is EXACT on this engine, not an estimate** — measured on a 1,000,000-row parquet file before choosing the design: `parquet_file_metadata(path).num_rows` (footer read, zero rows scanned) when the Probe attaches directly to a `format: parquet` Ingress with no `partition_filters`/`sandbox_limit`; a plain `COUNT(*)` otherwise (also sub-millisecond, measured, not assumed — 0.0007s bare, 0.0016s filtered). Spark samples this signal to dodge an expensive DISTRIBUTED action; that rationale does not transfer to a single-node exact count, so neither path is gated by `danger.allow_full_probe_actions` here (unlike `null_rates`/`value_distribution`/`distinct_count`, which stay gated exactly like Spark's). **`partition_stats` stays unimplemented** — DuckDB is single-process with no partition concept to report; it gets its own dedicated runtime warning (`runtime_probe_signal_unsupported`) rather than being silently swallowed by the generic "unknown signal type" path or faking a value. `config.probes.max_sample_rows`/`default_sample_fraction` and `config.danger.allow_full_probe_actions` move from `ignored_with_warning` to `supported` — they were previously accepted-but-inert on this engine purely because Probe itself did not exist; now genuinely threaded through `duckdb_/executor.py`'s Probe dispatch. `custom_probe_driver_code`'s compiler warning was worded in Spark terms ("driver", ".collect()/.count()") because it could never fire on DuckDB while Probe was unsupported there; reworded engine-neutral (the risk — arbitrary Python holding a live handle to the full relation/DataFrame — is identical on both engines) and un-gated so it fires on both. Verified end to end: a real Regulator gated by a real DuckDB Probe `threshold` signal through the real `Surveyor.evaluate_regulator` (no mocking) closes/opens correctly on both verdicts. Gallery snippets `15_regulator_timeout`, `18_probe_value_dist`, `21_probe_custom`, `26_probe_signals_all`, `27_self_healing`, `33_runtime_functions`, `43_probe_report_stdout` — previously `UNSUPPORTED` on the DuckDB lane purely because of Probe — now run. `feature.parallel_mode` and `egress.mode.overwrite_partitions` were re-examined and left `unsupported` — both already carried an accurate, examined hint (a measured `DuckDBPyConnection` thread-safety finding; partition-directory-pruning scope, respectively) rather than an unexamined verdict, so neither needed a change. - **Egress `on_new_columns` (`allow`/`alert`/`fail`) now runs on the DuckDB engine (Pass F).** Carried no examined reasoning for its `unsupported` verdict — investigating found the implementation is genuinely small: reading an existing target's column set is a zero-scan `LIMIT 0` metadata read (the same reader `duckdb_/ingress.py` already uses for its own `schema_hint`/`on_new_columns` checks), so this needs no Delta/Iceberg transaction log the way Spark's `mergeSchema` framing suggests — `fail` raises before the write, `alert` logs + records a runtime warning (`runtime_egress_new_columns`) then proceeds, `allow` proceeds silently (`COPY ... TO` always writes a fresh file with whatever columns the incoming relation has). New `_enforce_on_new_columns` in `aqueduct/executor/duckdb_/egress.py`. `egress.on_new_columns.{allow,alert,fail}` and `egress.field.on_new_columns` flip from `unsupported` to `supported`. Gallery snippet `30_ingress_schema_hints`'s write-side `on_new_columns: allow` now runs on the DuckDB lane. (`aqueduct/executor/duckdb_/egress.py`, `aqueduct/executor/duckdb_/capabilities.yml`, `docs/compatibility.md` regenerated; tests: `tests/test_executor_duckdb/test_executor.py`)
- **`format: depot` Egress runs on the DuckDB engine (Pass E item 1).** `depot: format` writes a single KV entry via the `DepotStore` Python interface (`depot.put(key, value)`) — a plain Python call, never routed through any engine's SQL/relation layer, so the only DuckDB-specific piece was `value_expr`'s single opt-in aggregate query (`rel.aggregate(expr).fetchone()`, this engine's equivalent of Spark's single `.collect()` action). New `_write_depot` in `aqueduct/executor/duckdb_/egress.py` mirrors Spark's `_write_depot` exactly. `egress.format.depot` and its `key`/`value`/`value_expr` fields flip to `supported`. Gallery snippet `31_depot_kv` now runs and passes on the DuckDB lane. (`aqueduct/executor/duckdb_/egress.py`, `aqueduct/executor/duckdb_/capabilities.yml`; tests: `tests/test_executor_duckdb/test_executor.py::test_write_egress_format_depot_value`, `test_write_egress_format_depot_value_expr`)
- **`feature.python_udf` runs on the DuckDB engine (Pass E item 2).** New `aqueduct/executor/duckdb_/udf.py` registers Python UDFs via `conn.create_function()`. Verified, not assumed: the default `type="native"` execution really is row-at-a-time (a per-row call log shows one call per input row, not a batch — proven by `test_row_at_a_time_not_batched`), and `null_handling="special"` is set explicitly because DuckDB's own default silently skips the call on a NULL argument instead of invoking the function (the opposite of Spark's Python UDFs, which always invoke the function and let the body decide — exactly the `mask_email(email: str | None)` contract gallery snippet `05_channel_sql_udf` relies on). Exception propagation is DuckDB's own default (an uncaught exception aborts the query, matching an uncaught exception aborting a Spark action). `return_type` renders through the same Arrow type hub as `schema_hint`/`schema_match`. Parameterized (factory) UDFs via `params:` work identically to Spark's contract. User code loads through `aqueduct.infra.module_loading.load_module` with `manifest.base_dir` — never a bare `importlib.import_module` (the exact bug AGENTS.md records shipping independently five times). `deterministic` (default `true`) now maps onto DuckDB's `side_effects` (inverted) — a strict improvement, not a divergence, since Spark's own `udf.py` never reads this field either (a pre-existing, cross-engine gap left as-is; not fixed here — see the "Related findings, not fixed" note). `lang: java`/`scala` UDFs stay honestly `unsupported` (`feature.java_udf`) — DuckDB is not on the JVM. Gallery snippet `05_channel_sql_udf` now runs and passes on the DuckDB lane, both the static and the parameterized UDF path. (`aqueduct/executor/duckdb_/udf.py`, `aqueduct/executor/duckdb_/executor.py`, `aqueduct/executor/duckdb_/capabilities.yml`; tests: `tests/test_executor_duckdb/test_udf.py`)
- **The Assert module now runs on the DuckDB engine — every rule type and every `on_fail` action, no partial verdicts.** Assert was declared `unsupported` on DuckDB purely as a scope decision (`module.type.Assert`'s hint said outright "the DuckDB handlers cover Ingress/Channel/Junction/Funnel/Egress only"); there was no semantic obstacle — every rule is a SQL aggregate or a boolean/filter expression, which DuckDB does natively. New `aqueduct/executor/duckdb_/assert_.py` mirrors `spark/assert_.py`'s semantics rule-by-rule — `schema_match`, `min_rows`, `max_rows`, `freshness`, `not_null`, `sql`, `sql_row`, `custom`, `spillway_rate`, plus `null_rate` (a schema-legal rule type Spark's own dispatcher only ever recognized via a raw string literal, never promoted to its `AssertRuleType` enum — load-bearing for gallery snippet `12_assert_null_sampling`, which uses it directly) — and all 5 `on_fail` actions (`abort`, `warn`, `webhook`, `quarantine`, `trigger_agent`), translated into DuckDB's own idiom: one batched `rel.aggregate()` query for the aggregate rules, one sampled `USING SAMPLE ... (bernoulli)` query for `null_rate`, lazy `rel.filter()`/`rel.project()` for row-level rules and quarantine stamping. `sql`/`sql_row` expressions are sqlglot-transpiled from Spark SQL to DuckDB SQL (same idiom as `duckdb_/channel.py`'s `op: sql`), `schema_match`'s expected types go through the same Arrow type-hub translation `duckdb_/ingress.py`'s `schema_hint` validation already uses (so a schema written against Spark's `simpleString()` vocabulary validates on DuckDB unmodified), `custom` resolves `fn:` through `infra/module_loading.load_callable` (never a bare import — the exact bug class `infra/module_loading.py`'s docstring says shipped independently 5 times), and `quarantine` reuses the existing `feature.spillway`/spillway-port machinery rather than a second path. Wired into `duckdb_/executor.py`'s dispatch loop and topo sort (Assert was previously excluded from both, alongside Probe, which stays genuinely unsupported). Every `assert*` capability leaf (`module.type.Assert`, `assert.field.rules`, `assert_on_fail.field.*`, the full `assert_rule.field.*` family) flips from `unsupported` to `supported`. (`aqueduct/executor/duckdb_/assert_.py` [new], `aqueduct/executor/duckdb_/executor.py`, `aqueduct/executor/duckdb_/capabilities.yml`, `docs/specs.md` §10.9 + Assert section, `docs/compatibility.md` regenerated; tests: `tests/test_executor_duckdb/test_assert.py` [new, 28 tests], `tests/test_capabilities/test_gate_module_and_feature.py`)
- **`engine.duckdb.s3_endpoint`/`s3_url_style`/`s3_use_ssl` — the non-AWS-S3-compatible escape hatch, verified end-to-end against a real MinIO instance (closes the untested S3 claim from the httpfs/Q4 work).** DuckDB's httpfs+`CREATE SECRET` S3 wiring shipped without ever being round-tripped against a guaranteed S3-compatible endpoint. Measured against a real MinIO instance: AWS's default virtual-hosted addressing (`bucket.s3.amazonaws.com`) and TLS-everywhere assumption don't hold for MinIO — the SAME credentials against the SAME bucket fail with only `s3_endpoint` omitted, regardless of which secret is configured. Three new literal (not secret) fields on `DuckDBEngineConfig` — `s3_endpoint` (`host:port`, no scheme), `s3_url_style` (`vhost`|`path`), `s3_use_ssl` (bool) — feed DuckDB's `CREATE SECRET (TYPE S3, ..., ENDPOINT ..., URL_STYLE ..., USE_SSL ...)`, omitted from the statement (leaving DuckDB's AWS defaults untouched) when unset. `configure_s3_secret`/`resolve_s3_secret_from_config` (`aqueduct/executor/duckdb_/extensions.py`) changed shape to accommodate the three new fields — `resolve_s3_secret_from_config` now returns a dict (`key_id`/`secret`/`region`/`endpoint`/`url_style`/`use_ssl`) instead of a 3-tuple, so callers do `configure_s3_secret(conn, **result)`; both call sites (`aqueduct/executor/duckdb_/engine.py::_make_session`, `aqueduct/doctor/__init__.py::_probe_duckdb_handoff`) updated, no back-compat shim (per the standing "a breaking change ships as documentation" rule — this is an internal helper, not a public contract). Verified: (1) DuckDB Ingress/Egress read+write round-trip against a real `s3://`/`s3a://` MinIO bucket; (2) a cross-engine `handoff.root` on a real MinIO root, BOTH directions (Spark island → DuckDB island and the reverse) — the case that made this matter, since Spark writes the spill from its executors and the next engine reads it back, so both sides must reach the same URI with their own config; (3) `aqueduct doctor`'s `handoff-access:duckdb` check genuinely passes against a reachable MinIO root and fails informatively (names the URL, the real HTTP 404) against an unreachable one. Spark's side needs no new Aqueduct code — `engine.spark.conf`'s existing `spark.jars.packages`/`spark.hadoop.fs.s3a.*` keys already cover it — but two settled, worth-recording facts: `hadoop-aws`'s version must match the installed PySpark's bundled `hadoop-client` jar exactly (a mismatched patch version raises `NoSuchMethodError` on READ, not on write), and any URI touched by Spark must use `s3a://`, not `s3://` (DuckDB's httpfs accepts either identically, so `s3a://` is the one scheme safe for both engines or a shared `handoff.root`). New capability leaves declared `supported` on DuckDB (positional — Spark declares no verdict for another engine's `engine.<name>.*` fields, per Q4's positional scoping). (`aqueduct/config.py`, `aqueduct/executor/duckdb_/extensions.py`, `aqueduct/executor/duckdb_/engine.py`, `aqueduct/doctor/__init__.py`, `aqueduct/executor/duckdb_/capabilities.yml`, `aqueduct/templates/default/aqueduct.yml.template`, `docs/production_guide.md`, `docs/specs.md` §10.4.3, `docs/compatibility.md` regenerated; tests: `tests/test_executor_duckdb/test_engine_config.py`, `tests/test_executor_duckdb/test_s3_minio.py` [new], `tests/test_executor_duckdb/test_handoff_s3_minio.py` [new] + its subprocess worker `_handoff_s3_minio_worker.py` [new, not collected by pytest — see the module docstring for why the Spark side of this test must run in a fresh subprocess], `tests/conftest.py` [`requires_minio`/`ensure_minio_bucket`, same skip-cleanly-when-unreachable pattern as `requires_postgres`/`requires_redis`])
- **`scripts/run_snippets.sh` gets a fourth result state, `UNSUPPORTED`, and a per-engine mode (gallery snippet harness overhaul).** The harness only ever knew PASS/FAIL/SKIP; running it against a second registered engine (DuckDB) collapsed two genuinely different situations into one bucket — a snippet the harness deliberately doesn't run (`SKIP`, a human decision) and a snippet the TARGET ENGINE cannot run at all (a compile-time `CompileError` from the capability gate, `aqueduct/compiler/capability_check.py`) — so neither a verdict flipping to `supported` nor a real regression would show up distinctly. `UNSUPPORTED` is detected from the exact `"Engine capability gate failed"` `CompileError` text the gate raises, never a hand-curated "doesn't work on engine X" list, and the summary names the leaf(s) that caused it (`unsupported_leaves`). Only `FAIL` fails the run — `SKIP` and `UNSUPPORTED` stay informational, load-bearing for the blocking `snippets-lts` lane. New `-e/--engine ENGINE` flag forwards to every `aqueduct run` as `--set deployment.engine=ENGINE` (the script passing through the CLI's existing `--set`, not a new user-facing concept); omitted, behavior is unchanged (each snippet runs on whatever its own `aqueduct.yml` declares). The GitHub Actions step summary (`gh_summary_row`) renders all four states with distinct markers (`:white_check_mark:`/`:x:`/`:heavy_minus_sign:`/`:no_entry_sign:`) and carries the engine per row so a matrix run's Spark and DuckDB rows are distinguishable. `version-matrix.yml`'s `snippets` (canary) and `snippets-lts` (blocking) jobs gain `strategy.matrix.engine: [spark, duckdb]`, run_snippets.sh's `-e` forwarding the matrix value — the canary stays unpinned/uncached/`continue-on-error`, `snippets-lts` stays pinned to `requirements/compat-py3.11.txt` and blocking, per snippet, unchanged from before this change. `tests/test_meta_ci.py::test_snippets_lanes_cover_every_registered_engine` (new) fails the build if either job's `strategy.matrix.engine` is missing a registered engine, resolved from `CAPABILITY_REGISTRY`, never hardcoded. Measured on a clean checkout, full 44-snippet runs: the DuckDB lane's 24 `UNSUPPORTED` snippets fail at compile in 2.4s-4.3s each (no JVM boot at all), so the DuckDB lane's total wall-clock (~7.5 minutes, summed per-snippet durations) is materially cheaper than Spark's, whose per-snippet JVM-boot cost holds around 30-38s regardless of blueprint complexity — confirmed by measurement, not assumed. (`scripts/run_snippets.sh`, `.github/workflows/version-matrix.yml`, `tests/test_meta_ci.py`, `AGENTS.md`, `docs/compatibility.md`)
- **Universal `timezone:` config key + the `duration(unit)` hub type (cross-engine handoff remediation batch C).** Two additions, both existing only because a Blueprint can now span more than one engine (Phase 81/82). A new top-level `aqueduct.yml` key, `timezone:` (e.g. `"UTC"`), is applied to EVERY registered engine's session at creation — resolved through the engine registry, never a hardcoded list — Spark's `spark.sql.session.timeZone` (`aqueduct/executor/spark/session.py::make_spark_session`) and DuckDB's `SET TimeZone=?` (`aqueduct/executor/duckdb_/engine.py::_make_session`). Engine-native tz settings already worked standalone (`engine.spark.conf.spark.sql.session.timeZone`); the shared key only earns its keep once TWO engines run in one Blueprint, where a divergent per-engine session time zone silently changes `to_timestamp()`/cast results between islands — the same reasoning as the `outputTimestampType` fix below, applied to session timezone instead of Parquet encoding. Precedence: an explicit engine-native override always wins for that engine (Spark applies the universal value only when `spark.sql.session.timeZone` is absent from the resolved `engine.spark.conf`), and a divergence between the two fires a suppressible warning (`engine_timezone_conflict`) naming both values — the whole point of the universal key is making cross-engine timezone drift VISIBLE, never silently letting one engine drift. `SessionSpec` (`aqueduct/executor/protocol.py`) gains a `timezone` field threaded through every session-construction call site: the single-engine run path, `run_polyglot()`, and the patch-preview sandbox gate (`run_sandbox_gate`/`_run_patch_gates_inline`, all their callers). DuckDB has no `engine.duckdb.*` conf knob yet, so the precedence fork is exercised on Spark today.

  New hub type constructor `duration(unit)` (`unit` one of `s`/`ms`/`us`/`ns` — Arrow's own `TimeUnit` granularities), the seventeenth (`aqueduct/typehub.py::Duration`). Deliberately **integer-backed**, not modeled on either engine's native `INTERVAL` type: Spark's day-time `INTERVAL` and DuckDB's `INTERVAL` do not share a Parquet representation either engine's writer/reader can round-trip against the other, so a hub constructor built on either one would inherit that same cross-engine fragility. `render_type` renders `duration(unit)` as a plain `bigint`/`BIGINT` on both engines instead (`aqueduct/executor/spark/type_render.py`, `aqueduct/executor/duckdb_/type_render.py`) — a signed 64-bit integer has no logical-type ambiguity to disagree about, so it round-trips faithfully across a real Spark↔DuckDB handoff verified in both directions (unlike `timestamp_tz`, no session-factory fix was needed). Fixed a real defect found while wiring this in: `aqueduct/compiler/capability_check.py::_type_leaves_for_hub_type`'s fallback branch rendered a parametrized hub type's FULL spelling (including the argument) into its capability-leaf id, so a Channel cast to `duration(us)` minted a bogus `type.duration(us)` leaf with no row in either engine's `capabilities.yml` instead of the governed `type.duration` — a `Decimal` special case already existed for exactly this shape, `Duration` did not; both are now pinned by regression tests (`tests/test_capabilities/test_gate_module_and_feature.py`). `type.duration: supported` on both engines with real `tests:` links (`type.*` is an EXECUTION leaf under the capability framework). `config.timezone: supported` on both engines (`config.*` leaves are warn-only, no `tests:` requirement). Both engines' `capabilities.yml` now hold 294 rows (189 Blueprint-grammar + 105 engine-config, up from 261/160/101). (`aqueduct/config.py`, `aqueduct/typehub.py`, `aqueduct/executor/protocol.py`, `aqueduct/executor/spark/session.py`, `aqueduct/executor/spark/engine.py`, `aqueduct/executor/spark/type_render.py`, `aqueduct/executor/duckdb_/engine.py`, `aqueduct/executor/duckdb_/type_render.py`, `aqueduct/executor/orchestrator.py`, `aqueduct/compiler/capability_check.py`, `aqueduct/cli/run.py`, `aqueduct/cli/__init__.py`, `aqueduct/cli/patch.py`, `aqueduct/patch/preview.py`, `aqueduct/executor/spark/capabilities.yml`, `aqueduct/executor/duckdb_/capabilities.yml`, `docs/specs.md` §9.1/§9.3/§10.1/§10.3.1/§10.9 → Version 2.38, `docs/extending.md`, `docs/compatibility.md`, `SKILL.md`, `aqueduct/templates/default/aqueduct.yml.template`, `aqueduct/templates/default/blueprints/blueprint.yml.template`; tests: `tests/test_typehub/test_typehub.py`, `tests/test_executor/test_executor_session.py`, `tests/test_executor/test_executor_channel.py`, `tests/test_executor_duckdb/test_executor.py`, `tests/test_capabilities/test_gate_module_and_feature.py`)
- **`aqueduct run` now actually runs a polyglot Blueprint — routing, healing, and rendering (cross-engine handoff CLI wiring).** `run_polyglot()` (2.36) was a complete, independently-tested runtime unit with no way to reach it from the CLI; `aqueduct/cli/run.py`'s healing loop now routes a Manifest with more than one island through it (`run_polyglot(..., record_result=False)`) in place of the single-engine `execute()` call, via a new `_execute_target()` seam used at every `execute()` call site in the file. A single-engine Manifest (`len(manifest.islands) <= 1`, the existing default) takes the EXACT same path it always has — no eager per-island session build, no change to kwargs, output, or exit codes; the whole existing CLI test suite is the compatibility check and passes unchanged. `run_polyglot()` gains `record_result` (default `True`, preserving its own prior standalone contract) so the CLI can call `surveyor.record(result, exc=..., engine=result.failed_engine)` itself and get the returned `FailureContext` to drive its own heal decision, plus a new `ExecutionResult.failed_engine` field (additive, `None` for every single-engine result) naming the failing ISLAND rather than the run's nominal `deployment.engine`. Every `generate_agent_patch`/`generate_cascade_patch`/progressive-chain call site now resolves the healing prompt's engine from `failure_ctx.engine` instead of the nominal default, so the LLM gets the failing island's persona/rules (`ExecutorProtocol.prompt_rules`) and the `patch_index`/`healed_by` provenance record names the engine that actually produced the fix. `run_polyglot()` also gained an `AqueductError` wrap around each island's `execute()` call (both engines' `ExecuteError` subclass it) so a structural failure — a cycle, a bad `--from`/`--to`, a missing resume checkpoint — that RAISES rather than returning a `ModuleResult` still gets island/engine attribution and spill-lifecycle bookkeeping instead of escaping uncaught; and forwards `checkpoint_root`/`block_full_actions` (COMMON `ExecutorProtocol.execute` kwargs) plus `parallel`/`use_observe`/`sampling`/`explain_capture` (OPTIONAL kwargs, via `call_execute()`'s existing per-engine filter-and-warn) to every island, closing a real gap where those flags/config would have silently had no effect on a polyglot run. `resume_run_id` is NOT forwarded to a non-skipped island's own `execute()` (only used for the cross-engine handoff-spill resume decision) — both engines' executors treat a non-empty `resume_run_id` as "a checkpoint must exist for THIS island under this run_id" and raise otherwise, the common case for an island that never checkpointed under the failed prior run; tried once, reverted, documented in the module docstring. `--from`/`--to` refuse a polyglot Manifest outright (`CONFIG_ERROR`) rather than attempting an undefined cross-island module range. Lifecycle hooks pass `session=None` for a polyglot run (no single live session survives to hook time), which hits hooks' existing subprocess fallback; a `blueprint:` hook entry with `in_process: true` now gets a `[hook_in_process_unavailable]` warning naming why, instead of silently changing execution model with no signal. `run_sandbox_gate` (Gate 3) and `--sandbox` both refuse a polyglot Manifest (a single-session replay can only ever validate ONE of the Blueprint's engines, which is worse than no replay at all) — Gate 3 returns `skip` and the run prints the reason at the moment a patch applies without it (one-shot per run), `--sandbox` exits `CONFIG_ERROR`. Rendering: the run header names every engine involved (`spark+duckdb (2 islands)`) instead of the single nominal default; every module's transcript line carries its own resolved engine (islands.py's per-module resolution, already stamped onto the compiled Manifest); a synthetic Handoff module's result renders as a first-class step (`⇄ from → to (engineA→engineB)`, bytes transferred, duration) instead of being misparsed into the Arcade-child tree nesting its `__`-containing id would otherwise trigger. `report --format json` gains a top-level `engines` list and a per-module `engine` field on every `module_results` entry, persisted by `Surveyor.record()` unconditionally (present, and identical across modules, for a single-engine run too). One known v1 cost, recorded rather than built: each heal iteration calls `run_polyglot()` fresh, rebuilding every island's session on a retry instead of reusing one across iterations the way the single-engine path reuses its one session for the whole run — the same "not yet optimized" framing `run_polyglot()` already uses for same-engine session recurrence within a run. (`aqueduct/cli/run.py`, `aqueduct/cli/hooks.py`, `aqueduct/cli/output.py`, `aqueduct/executor/orchestrator.py`, `aqueduct/executor/models.py`, `aqueduct/patch/preview.py`, `aqueduct/surveyor/surveyor.py`, `aqueduct/cli/observability.py`, `docs/specs.md` §4.2/§8.2/§10.9 → Version 2.37, `docs/roadmap.md`; tests: `tests/test_cli/test_cli_polyglot_run.py` [new], `tests/test_executor/test_orchestrator_e2e.py`, `tests/test_patch/test_patch_preview_sandbox_engine.py`, `tests/test_cli/test_cli_heal_apply_callback.py`)
- **Runtime execution of a polyglot Blueprint — engine-native transport, per-island session lifecycle, spill lifecycle, and observability (cross-engine handoff Step 3).** A compiled multi-island Manifest can now actually RUN, not just compile. New `aqueduct/executor/orchestrator.py::run_polyglot()` is a coordinator layered above the existing single-engine `ExecutorProtocol.execute()` calls: it opens a session LAZILY per island in topological order (an island depends on every island whose Handoff output it reads), executes that island's own sub-Manifest (built from `manifest.islands`/the Handoff modules bridging them, `_sub_manifest`), and closes the session immediately after — a deliberate v1 choice, not yet optimized, that a recurring engine (`spark -> duckdb -> spark`) opens two separate sessions rather than keeping one alive across the gap. One `run_id` covers the whole run regardless of island count; `run_id` was never an engine's own session/app id and this does not change that. A single-engine Manifest (always exactly one island) runs through the same function, unchanged — a strict superset, not a special case. Each Handoff module now does REAL engine-native parquet transport instead of being a compile-time-only placeholder: `df.write.parquet`/`spark.read.parquet` on Spark (`aqueduct/executor/spark/executor.py`), `COPY ... TO ... (FORMAT PARQUET)`/`read_parquet` on DuckDB (`aqueduct/executor/duckdb_/executor.py`) — no new backend abstraction, no `fsspec` in the write/read path, dispatching on which side of the boundary (write vs. read) the current island's sub-Manifest contains. New `aqueduct/executor/spill.py` owns the spill directory lifecycle AROUND that transport: `<root>/<manifest_hash>/<run_id>/<edge_id>/` layout, delete-on-success, keep-on-failure (`handoff.keep_on_failure`), and `sweep_orphan_spills()` — run at the start of every `run_polyglot()` call, reclaiming a `run_id` directory whose `run_records` status is terminal and not a kept failure (including a `run_id` with no `run_records` row at all — an "unknown" state, reclaimed per the function's own contract, a bug caught and fixed during this step's own test-writing: the initial implementation conflated "no row" with "still running" and left unknown runs un-swept forever). `resume_run_id` makes an island whose outgoing handoff already has a spilled directory under that prior run skip re-execution entirely (`status="skipped"`) and downstream islands read the prior spill — the resume story; fixed a related bug found while testing it, where a downstream island's execute() call was handed a freshly-computed (wrong, this-run) spill URI instead of the resumed run's actual directory when its upstream write side had been skipped. Two IO stacks touch one directory: engines write/read natively; Aqueduct's own cleanup needs `fsspec` for a remote `handoff.root`, and `run_polyglot()` emits a suppressible `handoff_cleanup_unavailable` warning at run start rather than letting spill accumulate silently when the root is remote and `fsspec` is absent. Observability: a Handoff module gets a `module_metrics` row (`bytes_written`/`bytes_read`/`duration_ms`, measured from spill directory size) on EITHER engine — DuckDB's first `module_metrics` write, scoped to Handoff only (hoisted, engine-agnostic `MODULE_METRICS_DDL`/`write_module_metrics`/`resolve_observability_store`/`manifest_hash` now live in `aqueduct/executor/models.py`, deduplicating what were previously separate private copies in each engine's executor). `Surveyor.record()` gains an optional `engine` override so a polyglot run's `FailureContext.engine` and structured error extraction reflect the island that actually failed, not the run's nominal `deployment.engine`; `compiler.py`'s cross-engine heal-patch provenance gate (`cross_engine_heal`) now checks once per distinct island engine rather than only the single deployment default. Capability verdict: `module.type.Handoff` flips from `unsupported` to `supported` on both shipped engines (real, tested transport now exists) — the leaf is still never actually consulted by the per-island capability gate (a Handoff module's id is deliberately kept OUT of every island's `module_ids`, exactly as before; `tests/test_compiler/test_islands.py::test_handoff_modules_never_reach_the_capability_gate` still passes unchanged, proving execution didn't require folding it into island membership). `docs/compatibility.md` regenerated. Not yet wired into `aqueduct run`'s existing single-engine CLI loop (healing, patch gates, retries, hooks, sandbox replay, run-header rendering) — tracked in `docs/roadmap.md`. **Type-fidelity check across the boundary (every hub constructor tested Spark↔DuckDB via real Parquet round trips):** all sixteen constructors round-trip correctly, including `timestamp_tz` — see the session-factory default change below, which is what makes the Spark write side of the boundary annotate the logical type correctly. `timestamp_ntz` and the reverse direction (DuckDB → Spark, either timestamp variant) round-trip correctly as well. (`aqueduct/executor/orchestrator.py`, `aqueduct/executor/spill.py`, `aqueduct/executor/models.py`, `aqueduct/executor/protocol.py`, `aqueduct/executor/spark/executor.py`, `aqueduct/executor/duckdb_/executor.py`, `aqueduct/executor/duckdb_/engine.py`, `aqueduct/surveyor/surveyor.py`, `aqueduct/compiler/compiler.py`, `aqueduct/compiler/models.py`, `aqueduct/models.py`, `aqueduct/executor/spark/capabilities.yml`, `aqueduct/executor/duckdb_/capabilities.yml`, `docs/specs.md` §10.9 → Version 2.36, `docs/observability_guide.md`, `docs/roadmap.md`, `docs/compatibility.md`; tests: `tests/test_executor/test_orchestrator.py`, `tests/test_executor/test_spill.py`, `tests/test_executor/test_orchestrator_e2e.py`).
- **Synthetic cross-engine handoff module insertion + the `handoff:` config block — compile-time synthesis only (cross-engine handoff Step 2).** Every boundary edge derived in Step 1 (`aqueduct.compiler.islands.find_boundary_edges`) now gets a real, synthetic Handoff module spliced in: `A -> B` becomes `A -> handoff -> B`, with the original edge's port preserved on both new edges (always `main` in v1 — a cross-island spillway edge is already a `CompileError`). New `aqueduct/compiler/handoff.py`: `insert_handoff_modules()` builds a collision-proof id (`<from>__handoff__<to>`, safe because `__` is reserved/rejected in authored module ids) and a `Module` carrying the full transport contract in `config` (`edge_id`, `from_module`/`to_module`, `from_engine`/`to_engine`, `port`) for the executor (a later step) to consume — no engine session is touched and no directory is created here. `ModuleType` gains a `Handoff` member (`aqueduct/parser/models.py`) that is explicitly **not authorable**: `ModuleSchema.validate_type` rejects `type: Handoff` in Blueprint YAML with a dedicated message. Every handoff `Module` carries `synthetic=True` (a new `Module` field mirroring `Edge.injected`) and `engine=None` (it bridges two engines rather than resolving to one). Disjoint different-engine components and single-engine Blueprints get zero boundary edges (Step 1's free lunch, unchanged) so `insert_handoff_modules` returns its inputs byte-identical — verified by an identity (`is`), not just equality, assertion. A new suppressible compile warning, rule id `cross_engine_handoff_io`, names every handoff point (`aqueduct.warnings.emit`, the same machinery as every other compiler warning) so the extra I/O a split introduces is visible before the run. `aqueduct/compiler/lineage.py` gains a passthrough lineage row per handoff module (columns cross unchanged, the same `_opaque_row` wildcard convention already used for lineage that can't resolve column-by-column) plus two new sqlglot-backed helpers used by it and by Step 1's UDF attribution. Capability-gate interaction: `module.type.Handoff` is declared `unsupported` on both shipped engines (honest — no engine has a real transport handler yet) but the verdict is inert — a handoff module's id is never a member of any island's `module_ids` (islands are derived from the pre-insertion graph), so the per-island gate excludes it from every check by construction, the same way a disabled module already is; a later phase gives this leaf a real per-engine verdict once the executor-side transport exists. New top-level `aqueduct.yml` block `handoff:` (`root` — any URI BOTH engines can read/write, default `.aqueduct/handoff`, remote schemes NOT rejected unlike `checkpoint_root`; `keep_on_failure` — default true) configures WHERE the spill lands; directory layout `<root>/<manifest_hash>/<run_id>/<edge_id>/` is documented now, resolved by the executor at run time (`root` from config, `manifest_hash`/`run_id` the executor already has, `edge_id` from the handoff module's own config) — the same "config threads to `execute()`, never baked into the Manifest" pattern `checkpoint_root` already uses. Parquet is a fixed transport detail; there is no format knob. (`aqueduct/compiler/handoff.py`, `aqueduct/compiler/compiler.py`, `aqueduct/compiler/lineage.py`, `aqueduct/parser/models.py`, `aqueduct/parser/schema.py`, `aqueduct/config.py`, `aqueduct/executor/spark/capabilities.yml`, `aqueduct/executor/duckdb_/capabilities.yml`, `docs/specs.md` §4.3/§10.4.3/§10.9 → Version 2.35, `SKILL.md`, `aqueduct/templates/default/aqueduct.yml.template`, `aqueduct/templates/default/blueprints/blueprint.yml.template`; tests: `tests/test_compiler/test_handoff.py`, updated assertions in `tests/test_compiler/test_islands.py`/`tests/test_compiler/test_udf_attribution.py` now that a real handoff module exists at a boundary). Explicitly OUT of scope, left for a later step: the executor-side write/read, per-island session lifecycle, `bytes_written`/duration metrics, and the orphan spill-directory sweep.
- **The fan-shape conformance matrix for cross-engine handoff (cross-engine remediation batch B).** Fan shapes across a boundary (a Junction whose branches land on different engines, a Funnel whose inputs arrive from different engines, a split that crosses the boundary twice before rejoining, a Probe attached adjacent to a handoff) were always ALLOWED by the islands/handoff machinery, but nothing had systematically exercised them against real engines. New `tests/test_executor/test_orchestrator_fan_shapes_e2e.py`, written once and parametrized over both registered engine pairs (`spark->duckdb`, `duckdb->spark`), asserts real end-to-end behavior for all four shapes — row counts and values, handoff modules present/absent exactly where expected, per-module engine attribution, and spill cleanup on success — plus the Probe/Assert colocation rule holding right at a boundary (a mismatched `engine:` pin near a handoff is still a `CompileError`). (`tests/test_executor/test_orchestrator_fan_shapes_e2e.py` [new], `tests/test_compiler/test_handoff.py`)
